This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}). A crossed random-effects analysis accounting for the multi-membership structure of shared judges confirms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance at $K{\\geq}5$ under this more conservative correction (Appendix~\\ref{app:crossed_re}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 7/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 7/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}). Monte Carlo calibration (1{,}000 permutations per condition) confirms the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative: the maximum null rejection rate is $1.6\\%$ at nominal $\\alpha{=}5\\%$ across all 36 conditions ($12 \\times 3$ panel sizes), so reported significance counts are lower bounds (Appendix~\\ref{app:monte_carlo}).\n\n\\subsection{The Diminishing Weakest Link Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[height=4.5cm]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12. Shaded region: $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher, max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{minipage}\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig5_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-condition standardized $\\hat{\\beta}$ with 95\\% CI for $\\eta_{\\max}$ (left) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (right) at $K{=}3$ (wild cluster bootstrap).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMeta-analytic pooling (DerSimonian--Laird random effects \\citealp{dersimonian1986meta}, across 12 conditions) quantifies the trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$ ($I^2{=}91.3\\%$), $0.102$ [$-0.055, 0.259$] at $K{=}5$ ($I^2{=}99.4\\%$), $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$ ($I^2{=}99.8\\%$); CI crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive: $0.356$ [$0.267, 0.445$] ($I^2{=}87.7\\%$), $0.436$ [$0.318, 0.553$] ($I^2{=}98.9\\%$), $0.401$ [$0.298, 0.503$] ($I^2{=}99.4\\%$); magnitudes are heterogeneous but direction is consistent. The high $I^2$ values indicate substantial between-condition heterogeneity; pooled estimates summarize the central trend but individual conditions vary widely. Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} displays per-condition effect sizes with confidence intervals (per-condition dominance in Figure~\\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}, Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_analysis}). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity specifications. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms the signal does not depend on measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.730$ [$0.718, 0.745$] ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.285$ [$0.270, 0.301$] at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap is $\\Delta{=}0.446$ [$0.430, 0.460$]. The positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under adaptive attack contrasts with the Condorcet Jury Theorem \\citep{condorcet1785essai}, where independence among voters is protective. Under adversarial manipulation, independent judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses; this reversal is consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,kariyappa2019improving}.\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$). The Bayesian $R^2$ near zero implies that panel composition is almost unpredictive under calibrated aggregation---the diversity and weakest-link predictors may only be relevant under specific aggregation rules rather than being intrinsic panel properties. Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). At $K{=}5$, all four methods converge to identical $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5}); at $K{=}7$, methods diverge again, with Bayesian neutralization returning while threshold voting strengthens (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 9/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

```text
1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Proof of Proposition~\ref{thm:monotonicity}}
4	\label{app:proof}
5	
6	The replaced judge $J_K'$ is \emph{pivotal} exactly when $t{-}1$ of the remaining $K{-}1$ judges have already failed. Formally, let $S = \sum_{j=1}^{K-1} X_j^a$ denote the number of failures among the shared judges. The panel fails if and only if the $K$-th judge also fails when $S = t{-}1$, or when $S \geq t$ regardless of the $K$-th judge:
7	\begin{align}
8	\mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a) &= \Pr(S \geq t) + \Pr(S = t{-}1) \cdot \Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \mid S = t{-}1).
9	\end{align}
10	Since $\Pr(X_{K'}^a {=} 1 \mid \mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \mathbf{x}_{-K}) \geq \Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \mid \mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \mathbf{x}_{-K})$ for all attacks $a$ and realizations $\mathbf{x}_{-K}$ (by hypothesis), the second term is weakly larger for $\mathcal{P}'$ than for $\mathcal{P}$ under every attack. Taking the supremum over attacks preserves the inequality: $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P}') \geq \mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P})$. The result holds under arbitrary inter-judge correlations and any attack space $\mathcal{A}$.
11	
12	\section{Robustness Correction Methods}
13	\label{app:robustness_methods}
14	
15	The 455 panels are constructed combinatorially from 15 shared judges: each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ panels, inducing correlated residuals across panel-level observations. Standard OLS assumes independent errors, potentially inflating significance. We apply three corrections.
16	
17	\paragraph{Clustered Standard Errors.} Because each panel of $K{=}3$ judges does not map to a single cluster, we perform three separate one-way clusterings (one for each judge position in the ordered panel tuple) using CR1 bias-corrected sandwich estimators (13 clusters per position). The reported standard error for each coefficient is the maximum across the three clusterings, yielding the most conservative inference.
18	
19	\paragraph{Mixed Effects.} For each of the three judge positions, we fit a linear mixed-effects model with a random intercept for the judge occupying that position, estimating $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ as fixed effects. The reported $p$-value for each coefficient is the maximum across the three models.
20	
21	\paragraph{Wild Cluster Bootstrap.} We perform $B{=}9{,}999$ bootstrap resamples using Rademacher weights, clustering by each of the three panel positions in turn and taking the maximum $p$-value across positions (the most conservative aggregation). This approach avoids distributional assumptions on the cluster structure.
22	
23	\section{Crossed Random Effects Analysis}
24	\label{app:crossed_re}
25	
26	The one-way clustering in Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_methods} treats each judge position independently, but panels exhibit \emph{crossed} random effects: judge $j$ may occupy position~1 in one panel and position~2 in another, inducing a multi-membership structure. At $K{=}7$, each judge appears in $\binom{14}{6} = 3{,}003$ of $6{,}435$ panels (46.7\%), making crossed membership the dominant source of residual correlation.
27	
28	We fit multi-membership cluster-robust variance estimators (CRVE) using a sandwich estimator with $t(G{-}1) = t(14)$ reference distribution, where $G{=}15$ is the number of judges. Table~\ref{tab:crossed_re} compares bootstrap and crossed RE significance.
29	
30	\begin{table}[h]
31	\centering
32	\small
33	\begin{tabular}{rcccc}
34	\toprule
35	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Bootstrap}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Crossed RE (FDR)}} \\
36	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5}
37	$\boldsymbol{K}$ & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \\
38	\midrule
39	3 & 4/12 & 3/12 & 0/12 & 1/12 \\
40	5 & 7/12 & 2/12 & \textbf{8/12} & 3/12 \\
41	7 & 8/12 & 1/12 & \textbf{8/12} & 2/12 \\
42	\bottomrule
43	\end{tabular}
44	\caption{Crossed random effects vs.\ wild cluster bootstrap significance (BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). Multi-membership CRVE with $t(14)$ distribution. At $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 8/12 significance under crossed RE, matching or exceeding bootstrap. At $K{=}3$, the conservative $t(14)$ distribution with only 455 panels produces attenuation to 0/12 (raw $p$-values: 5/12 significant).}
45	\label{tab:crossed_re}
46	\end{table}
47	
48	SE inflation increases with $K$ as judge overlap intensifies: $1.75$--$4.96{\times}$ at $K{=}3$, $6$--$12{\times}$ at $K{=}5$, $10$--$19{\times}$ at $K{=}7$. Despite this inflation, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 8/12 significance at $K{\geq}5$ because the effect size is large enough to survive the correction. The $K{=}3$ attenuation (0/12) reflects two compounding factors: the conservative $t(14)$ reference distribution penalizes small-sample inference, and the effect sizes at $K{=}3$ are insufficient to overcome the inflated SEs. Raw (uncorrected) $p$-values show 5/12 conditions significant at $K{=}3$, confirming that the attenuation reflects degrees-of-freedom conservatism rather than absent effects.
49	
50	\section{Extended Analysis}
51	\label{app:extended_analysis}
52	
53	\begin{figure}[t]
54	\centering
55	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf}
56	\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}
57	\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}
58	\end{figure}
59	
60	\begin{figure}[t]
61	\centering
62	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}
63	\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\eta_{\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\eta_{\max}$ diminishes substantially.}
64	\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}
65	\end{figure}
66	
67	\subsection{BFT Reversal Mechanism: Detailed Evidence}
68	\label{app:bft_reversal}
69	
70	Under standard OLS, the regression results (Table~\ref{tab:dominance}) show $\eta_{\max}$ significant in 10 of 12 conditions and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ positively associated with ASR in all 12. Non-independence correction attenuates both predictors: under wild cluster bootstrap, $\eta_{\max}$ retains 3/12 and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 4/12, with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ slightly ahead. The directional pattern (all $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ point estimates positive) persists uniformly. The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) sharpens this: under random faults, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive association with panel error survives all corrections (2/2 conditions, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), indicating a genuine relationship whose statistical confidence attenuates under adaptive attack (4/12 after correction).
71	
72	\begin{figure}[h]
73	\centering
74	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}
75	\caption{GAM bootstrap $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction at $K{=}3$ ($B{=}9999$). All 12 conditions show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction (88.9--100\% bootstrap rate). The Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$).}
76	\label{fig:gam_split}
77	\end{figure}
78	
79	Under corrected data (V2), all 12 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ OLS coefficients are positive. The previously reported negative ARC $\times$ sycophancy coefficient ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = -0.192$) was an artifact of error-as-tie handling; after correction, it becomes positive ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = +0.257$). GAM bootstrap confirms the uniform positive direction: 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (88.9--100\% positive rate) and 12/12 at $K{=}5$ (100\%).
80	
81	\subsection{Diversity--Capability Tradeoff}
82	\label{app:diversity_capability}
83	
84	The $r = 0.70$--$0.81$ correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ ($K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$) reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.
85	
86	
87	Family-level labels provide indirect evidence that capability proximity matters more than taxonomic grouping: family membership is not a significant predictor of panel vulnerability ($p = 0.179$ on MMLU, $p = 0.385$ on ARC), consistent with models from the same provider exhibiting heterogeneous vulnerability profiles that vary by capability tier rather than by shared architecture \citep{kuncheva2003measures}.
88	
89	\subsection{Aggregation Mechanism Details}
90	\label{app:aggregation_details}
91	
92	\begin{figure}[t]
93	\centering
94	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_aggregation_gradient.pdf}
95	\caption{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient distribution across aggregation methods ($K{=}3$). Majority and weighted voting: uniformly positive (12+/0$-$); Bayesian calibration: reduced predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$, 5+/1$-$).}
96	\label{fig:aggregation}
97	\end{figure}
98	
99	Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (5+/1$-$) and reduces overall predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$), but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12 under OLS), providing partial defense. The pattern reflects calibrated reliability weighting: by estimating each judge's confusion matrix on clean data, Bayesian aggregation downweights weak judges, reducing the diversity channel while the individual weakness channel remains active. Threshold voting amplifies both channels because any single dissenting judge vetoes the panel decision, making compositional factors highly predictive of attack success ($R^2{=}0.829$). Bayesian calibration requires a clean accuracy prior, and an adaptive attacker aware of this calibration could target the prior itself \citep{tambe2011security}.
100	
101	
102	\subsection{Aggregation at \texorpdfstring{$K{=}5$}{K=5}}
103	\label{app:aggregation_k5}
104	
105	Table~\ref{tab:aggregation_k5} extends the aggregation analysis to $K{=}5$ (3{,}003 panels) under wild cluster bootstrap with 5-position max-$p$. All four aggregation methods yield identical $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (7/12), with $\eta_{\max}$ uniformly low (2--3/12). The Bayesian attenuation observed at $K{=}3$ (5+/1$-$; Table~\ref{tab:aggregation}) does not persist: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ reaches 7/12 regardless of aggregation rule. Predictability still varies ($\bar{R}^2$: threshold 0.408, Bayesian 0.166), but the diversity channel operates uniformly across all methods. At $K{=}7$, however, methods diverge again: Bayesian neutralization returns (0/12) while threshold voting strengthens to 10/12 (Table~\ref{tab:aggregation_k7}).

145	
146	\subsection{Metric Robustness Under Partial Panel Information}
147	\label{app:oracle}
148	
149	The main analysis computes $\eta_{\max}$ with full knowledge of panel composition (all three judges known). In deployment, a practitioner selecting or auditing a panel may know only a subset of the constituent judges. We test whether $\eta_{\max}$ retains predictive power under such partial information by recomputing it from two of the three panel members: for each panel, we cycle through all $\binom{3}{1} = 3$ leave-one-out masks, compute $\eta_{\max}$ from the two known judges, and average the regression estimates across masking scenarios.
150	
151	Under the most conservative inference (partial information + clustered SE), $\eta_{\max}$ remains the sole significant predictor with $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.589$, achieving significance in 12/12 conditions, stronger than the oracle baseline (4/12 under clustered SE). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ loses all predictive power (0/12). The averaging over masking scenarios smooths estimation noise inherent in the three-judge oracle computation, strengthening the $\eta_{\max}$ signal. This demonstrates that $\eta_{\max}$ computed from partial panel information is robust to incomplete composition knowledge and retains (indeed improves) its predictive power for panel ASR.
152	
153	\section{Panel Selection Strategy Comparison}
154	\label{app:panel_selection}
155	
156	
157	Figure~\ref{fig:panel_design} compares three panel selection strategies: $\min(\eta_{\max})$ (select the panel minimizing weakest-judge vulnerability), random selection, and $\max(K_{\mathrm{eff}})$ (maximize diversity). The $\min(\eta_{\max})$ strategy achieves the lowest ASR in all 12 conditions, consistent with the weakest-link dominance documented in \S\ref{sec:dominance}.
158	
159	\section{Clustered Standard Error Analysis}
160	\label{app:forest_plot}
161	
162	Under clustered standard errors (clustering on the $K$ judge positions), $\eta_{\max}$ retains significance in 4/12 conditions while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ achieves significance in 0/12. This contrasts with OLS ($\eta_{\max}$ 10/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 12/12) and wild cluster bootstrap ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), illustrating how the choice of inference method modulates apparent significance at $K{=}3$ while coefficient direction remains positive (12/12) for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across all methods, with significance ranging from 0/12 to 12/12 (Figure~\ref{fig:robustness}).
163	
164	\section{Robustness Checks: Subsampling and Specification}
165	\label{app:robustness_checks}
166	
167	Table~\ref{tab:robustness_checks} summarizes regression results across model subsets and alternative specifications at $K{=}5$, testing whether the predictor shift is robust to collinearity reduction and specification choice.
168	
169	\begin{table}[h]
170	\centering
171	\small
172	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
173	\toprule
174	\textbf{Subset / Method} & $\boldsymbol{r}$ & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \textbf{Trans.} \\
175	\midrule
176	Full bootstrap       & 0.81 & 2/12 & 7/12 & \checkmark \\
177	API-only (9) clust.  & 0.85 & 8/12 & 3/12 & --- \\
178	Cap-matched A (5A+4L) & 0.50 & ---  & 9/12 & \checkmark\checkmark \\
179	Cap-matched B (6A+3L) & 0.73 & ---  & 2/12 & --- \\
180	Fractional logit     & 0.81 & 6/12 & 9/12 & \checkmark \\
181	\bottomrule
182	\end{tabular}
183	\caption{Predictor shift robustness at $K{=}5$ across model subsets and specifications. $r$: correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$. $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ columns show FDR-significant counts out of 12 conditions. Trans.: whether $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates $\eta_{\max}$. API-only models ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$) show partially collinear predictors; capability-matched subsamples ($r{=}0.50$) strengthen the shift. Fractional logit \citep{papke1996econometric} confirms OLS patterns with 98.6\% directional agreement (71/72 direction matches; the single discordance is $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}7$ for ARC $\times$ score manipulation).}
184	\label{tab:robustness_checks}
185	\end{table}
186	
187	The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$) demonstrates that elevated collinearity between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ partially inflates standard errors: both predictors achieve moderate significance but neither dominates, and the predictor shift does not replicate. Breaking collinearity through capability-matched subsampling (pool~A, $r{=}0.50$) strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, comparable to the full-pool 7/12. Pool~B ($r{=}0.73$) falls near the decorrelation threshold, and the shift weakens accordingly. Random 9-model subsampling (1{,}000 trials) confirms an inverted-U: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance rate peaks when the subsample includes 3--4 local models (maximizing capability spread) and drops when the pool is too homogeneous (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
188	
189	\section{Random Subsample Analysis}
190	\label{app:random_subsample}
191	
192	To test whether the predictor shift depends on model pool composition, we randomly sample 9 of 15 models (1{,}000 trials), construct all $\binom{9}{5}$ panels, and run the bootstrap analysis at $K{=}5$. We stratify by the number of local models ($n_{\mathrm{local}} \in \{0, \ldots, 6\}$) in each subsample.
193	
194	Under clustered standard errors, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is the dominant predictor in 51.4\% of subsamples (mean significance 1.85/12), with an inverted-U as a function of $n_{\mathrm{local}}$: subsamples with 3--4 local models (maximizing capability range) achieve the highest $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance rate, while all-API ($n_{\mathrm{local}}{=}0$) and heavily-local ($n_{\mathrm{local}}{\geq}5$) subsamples show reduced detection. Significance is attenuated relative to the full pool by smaller sample sizes (126 vs.\ 3{,}003 panels at $K{=}5$), but the shift direction holds in the majority of subsamples. This confirms that the shift's detectability depends on capability diversity in the model pool rather than pool size per se.
195	
196	\section{Attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ Stability}
197	\label{app:attacked_keff}
198	
199	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean (unattacked) pairwise MI (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), but used to predict vulnerability under attack. We verify this assumption by recomputing MI from judge outputs under each attack type and comparing clean vs.\ attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ via Spearman rank correlation across all panels.
200	
201	\begin{table}[h]
202	\centering
203	\small
204	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
205	\toprule
206	\textbf{Attack} & \textbf{MMLU $\rho$} & \textbf{ARC $\rho$} \\
207	\midrule
208	Score manipulation & 0.98 & 0.99 \\
209	Authority bias     & 0.95 & 0.97 \\
210	Sycophancy         & 0.91 & 0.92 \\
211	Verbosity bias     & 0.91 & 0.92 \\
212	Prompt injection   & 0.63 & 0.60 \\
213	Position bias      & 0.60 & 0.08 \\
214	\bottomrule
215	\end{tabular}
216	\caption{Spearman rank correlation between clean and attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across 455 panels ($K{=}3$). Four attack types preserve MI structure ($\rho{\geq}0.91$); prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\rho{\approx}0.60$--$0.63$); position bias substantially disrupts pairwise dependencies.}
217	\label{tab:attacked_keff}
218	\end{table}
219	
220	Four attack types (score manipulation, authority bias, sycophancy, verbosity bias) preserve MI structure with $\rho{\geq}0.91$ on both benchmarks, validating the use of clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ as a predictor. Prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\rho{\approx}0.60$--$0.63$), consistent with its mode of overriding evaluation instructions, which can alter pairwise agreement patterns. Position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC, $p{=}0.10$), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior in Table~\ref{tab:dominance} (sole non-significant $\eta_{\max}$ condition) and suggesting that positional reordering fundamentally changes inter-judge dependence rather than merely introducing noise.
221

For each of the 36 conditions ($3K \times 2$ benchmarks $\times$ $6$ attacks), we compute the robustness value (RV): the minimum strength an unobserved confounder must have, in terms of partial $R^2$ with both treatment and outcome, to reduce $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient to zero.

RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$ in 3--4/12 conditions across all $K$ values (4/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), indicating that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness to omitted variable bias is stable rather than monotonically increasing with panel size.

291	\section{Cross-Benchmark Prediction Robustness}
292	\label{app:cross_benchmark}
293	
294	To test whether the diversity--vulnerability association reflects structural panel properties rather than benchmark-specific artifacts, we use $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from one benchmark's pairwise MI to predict panel vulnerability on the other. Under wild cluster bootstrap (FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$), both directions achieve 17/18 sign-consistent conditions across $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (5/6 at $K{=}3$, 6/6 at $K{\geq}5$). The sole exception in both directions is $K{=}3$ position bias, where all judges share similar ASR (${\sim}0.5$), leaving insufficient heterogeneity for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ to discriminate. Cross-benchmark $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations are $r{\approx}0.76$--$0.78$, indicating that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ primarily captures panel-level compositional structure rather than benchmark-specific judge interactions. While sign consistency is symmetric, cross-prediction $R^2$ is moderately asymmetric (ARC$\to$MMLU generally higher), suggesting that coefficients estimated from ARC's more constrained binary format generalize conservatively to MMLU's richer four-way choice distribution.
295

Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_poolA} compares the full-pool results with Pool~A.

321	Pool~A's restricted sample (84 panels at $K{=}3$, 126 at $K{=}5$) substantially limits statistical power under wild cluster bootstrap. Table~\ref{tab:poolA_power} reports power at standard effect sizes ($n_{\mathrm{sim}}{=}1{,}000$).

334	\bottomrule
335	\end{tabular}
336	\caption{Pool~A bootstrap power ($n_{\mathrm{sim}}{=}1{,}000$, wild cluster bootstrap). MDE at 80\%: $K{=}3$, $\beta_{\mathrm{std}}{=}0.897$; $K{=}5$, not attainable (maximum 30.1\% at $\beta_{\mathrm{std}}{=}1.0$).}
337	\label{tab:poolA_power}
338	\end{table}
339	
340	Two implications follow. First, Pool~A at $K{=}5$ is fundamentally underpowered: the maximum attainable power (30.1\% at $\beta_{\mathrm{std}}{=}1.0$) never reaches 80\%. The 10/12 Cinelli--Hazlett success at $K{=}5$ therefore reflects large effect sizes surviving low power, strengthening the causal signal. Second, the low Cinelli--Hazlett RV at $K{=}3$ (2/12 exceeding the $\eta_{\max}$ benchmark) is partially explained by Pool~A's 84-panel design: MDE$_{\,80\%}{=}0.897$ means only effects exceeding ${\approx}0.9$ SD are detectable. Crucially, the full-pool $K{=}3 \to K{=}5$ significance increase ($4/12 \to 7/12$) is not a power artifact: Pool~A power at $K{=}5$ (max 30.1\%) is \emph{lower} than at $K{=}3$ (max 85.8\%), so the increase reflects growing effect sizes rather than inflated type~I error.
341	
342	\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}
343	\label{app:epsilon}
344	
345	The log-log specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\epsilon \in \{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Coefficient magnitudes show moderate sensitivity to $\epsilon$ at $K{=}3$ (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.10$ between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, with three conditions exhibiting $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sign changes), reflecting the influence of zero-ASR panels on the log transformation. At $K{=}5$, all $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient directions are preserved across $\epsilon$ values (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.073$ between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, all $p < 0.005$), confirming that the predictor shift finding is robust to this modeling choice. The fractional logit specification (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), which avoids the log+$\epsilon$ transformation entirely, provides 98.6\% directional concordance with the main OLS results (71/72 direction matches) (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}).
346	
347	\section{Temperature Sensitivity Pilot}
348	\label{app:temperature}
349	
350	We evaluate $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ stability across decoding temperatures $t \in \{0.0, 0.3, 0.7, 1.0\}$ using a 3-model pilot (GPT-4.1-nano, Gemini-3.5-Flash, Claude-3-Haiku; 300 clean samples per benchmark, 3 repeats). The maximum $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ variation across all four temperatures is $\Delta < 0.034$ (MMLU: 0.033, ARC: 0.027), confirming that decoding randomness does not meaningfully perturb pairwise MI structure. Clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computation is thus robust to temperature variation within the range used by standard API configurations.
351	
352	\section{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ Distribution Statistics}
353	\label{app:keff_dist}
354	
355	Table~\ref{tab:keff_dist} summarizes the distribution of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across panel sizes.
356

365	5 & 3{,}003 & 3.35 (0.42) & [2.16, 4.30] \\

368	\end{tabular}
369	\caption{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ distribution across panel sizes. Mean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ increases approximately linearly with $K$ but remains below the theoretical maximum ($K_{\mathrm{eff}} = K$ for fully independent judges), reflecting non-zero inter-judge correlations.}
370	\label{tab:keff_dist}

372	
373	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits sufficient variance at each $K$ for regression analysis (coefficient of variation 10--14\%), with no floor or ceiling effects that would compress the predictor range.
374	
375	\section{Power Analysis Across Panel Sizes}
376	\label{app:power_analysis}
377	
378	Table~\ref{tab:mde} reports minimum detectable effect sizes (MDE at 80\% power) under wild cluster bootstrap for each $K$. The sharp decrease in MDE from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$ (0.144 to 0.048) reflects both increased sample size ($455 \to 3{,}003$ panels) and more clusters per position. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial significance at $K{=}3$ (4/12) is consistent with moderate power at this sample size; the 7/12 significance at $K{=}5$ is not type~I error inflation, as the design can detect effects as small as 0.048 SD.
379

395	
396	\section{Alternative Diversity Measures}
397	\label{app:alt_diversity}
398	
399	We compare $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ against three classical ensemble diversity measures using the same OLS specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) with FDR correction across 12 conditions pooled over $K$ values.
400	
401	\begin{table}[h]
402	\centering
403	\small
404	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
405	\toprule
406	\textbf{Measure} & \textbf{Sig} & \textbf{Dir.} & \textbf{$|\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}|$} & $\boldsymbol{\rho}$ \textbf{w/} $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\
407	\midrule
408	$Q$-statistic    & 9/12  & 6+/3$-$ & 0.251 & 0.599 \\
409	Disagreement     & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$ & 0.481 & 0.934 \\
410	$\kappa$-diversity & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$ & 0.497 & 0.978 \\
411	\addlinespace
412	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (ref.) & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$ & 0.430 & --- \\
413	\bottomrule
414	\end{tabular}
415	\caption{Comparison of diversity measures as predictors of panel ASR (OLS, FDR-corrected, 12 conditions pooled across $K$). Sig: FDR-significant conditions. $|\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}|$: mean absolute standardized coefficient. $\rho$: Pearson correlation with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$.}
416	\label{tab:alt_diversity}
417	\end{table}
418	
419	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} are near-equivalent ($\rho{=}0.978$), with comparable significance counts (12/12 vs.\ 11/12) and effect sizes (0.430 vs.\ 0.497). Disagreement rate achieves similar predictive power ($\rho{=}0.934$, 11/12). The $Q$-statistic is the weakest predictor (9/12, $|\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}|{=}0.251$), consistent with its known insensitivity to the type of error correlation that is associated with panel vulnerability \citep{kuncheva2003measures}. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s advantage over these alternatives is not predictive power but interpretability: it quantifies the number of effective independent channels in the panel, connecting directly to the information-theoretic foundation of panel aggregation (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
420	
421	\section{$\kappa$-Diversity Predictor Shift Comparison}
422	\label{app:kappa_regime}
423	
424	To test whether the predictor shift (\S\ref{sec:cross_k}) is specific to $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ or a generic property of diversity measures, we repeat the wild cluster bootstrap analysis replacing $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ with $\kappa$-diversity ($1 - \bar{\kappa}$, where $\bar{\kappa}$ is the mean pairwise Cohen's $\kappa$ on clean winner labels; $\rho{=}0.978$ with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$).
425	
426	\begin{table}[h]
427	\centering
428	\small
429	\begin{tabular}{rcccc}
430	\toprule
431	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{$\kappa$-diversity}} \\
432	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5}
433	$\boldsymbol{K}$ & \textbf{div.\ sig} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} & \textbf{div.\ sig} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} \\
434	\midrule
435	3 & 4/12  & 3/12 & 8/12 & 1/12 \\
436	5 & 7/12 & 2/12 & 7/12 & 4/12 \\
437	7 & 8/12  & 1/12 & 6/12 & 1/12 \\
438	\bottomrule
439	\end{tabular}
440	\caption{Predictor shift comparison: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\ $\kappa$-diversity across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a clear shift ($4 \to 7 \to 8$); $\kappa$-diversity significance decreases monotonically ($8 \to 7 \to 6$), showing no predictor shift.}
441	\label{tab:kappa_regime}
442	\end{table}
443	
444	$\kappa$-diversity is a stronger predictor than $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\ 4/12), consistent with its larger mean effect size ($|\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}|{=}0.497$ vs.\ 0.430; Table~\ref{tab:alt_diversity}). However, $\kappa$-diversity shows no predictor shift: its significance \emph{decreases} from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}7$ ($8 \to 7 \to 6$), the opposite of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s pattern ($4 \to 7 \to 8$). Furthermore, under $\kappa$-diversity, $\eta_{\max}$ retains significance at $K{=}5$ (4/12), whereas under $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ it drops to 2/12. The predictor shift---co-determination at $K{=}3$ transitioning to diversity dominance at $K{\geq}5$---is thus specific to $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s MI-based formulation, which captures effective judge independence rather than pairwise label agreement.
445	
446	\section{Shannon MI Entropy Predictor Shift Comparison}
447	\label{app:shannon_entropy}
448	
449	Shannon MI entropy ($H_{\mathrm{MI}} = \binom{K}{2}^{-1} \sum_{i<j} \mathrm{MI}(i,j)$) averages raw pairwise mutual information, measuring mean non-redundancy rather than effective independence. It correlates negatively with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ ($r = -0.63$ to $-0.76$ across $K$): panels with high mean MI tend to have lower effective independent channels, because $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI})$ transform penalizes high MI more aggressively than linear averaging.
450	
451	\begin{table}[h]
452	\centering
453	\small
454	\begin{tabular}{rcc}
455	\toprule
456	$\boldsymbol{K}$ & \textbf{Shannon MI sig} & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \textbf{sig} \\
457	\midrule
458	3 & 4/12 & 6/12 \\
459	5 & 4/12 & 8/12 \\
460	7 & 0/12 & 2/12 \\
461	\bottomrule
462	\end{tabular}
463	\caption{Predictor shift comparison: Shannon MI entropy vs.\ $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (within-framework wild cluster bootstrap, $B{=}999$, Webb weights, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). Shannon is flat then collapses ($4 \to 4 \to 0$); $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises before declining ($6 \to 8 \to 2$). Bootstrap parameters differ from the primary analysis ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher); absolute counts are not comparable with Table~\ref{tab:kappa_regime}.}
464	\label{tab:shannon_shift}
465	\end{table}
466	
467	Shannon MI entropy shows no predictor shift: significance is flat at $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$ (4/12) before collapsing at $K{=}7$ (0/12). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a clear rise from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$ ($6 \to 8$) within the same framework. Combined with the $\kappa$-diversity comparison (Appendix~\ref{app:kappa_regime}, $8 \to 7 \to 6$), two alternative diversity measures independently fail to exhibit the predictor shift, providing converging evidence that the shift is specific to $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s transformation of pairwise MI into an effective channel count.
468	
469	\section{Gaussian Copula Assumption and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ Robustness}
470	\label{app:copula_gof}
471	
472	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ admits a closed-form expression $K - \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i}\sum_{j \neq i} \rho_{ij}^2$ under the Gaussian copula, where $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI}(i,j)) = 1 - \rho_{ij}^2$ \citep{nelsen2006copulas}. We test whether this parametric assumption holds for judge pairs using the \citet{genest2008validity} Cram\'{e}r--von Mises goodness-of-fit test for Gaussian copulas.
473	
474	\paragraph{GoF Results.} Across all $\binom{15}{2} = 105$ judge pairs, the Gaussian copula is rejected at $\alpha{=}0.05$ for 97--100\% of pairs (MMLU: 102/105, 97.1\%; ARC: 104/105, 99.0\%). The rejections are not marginal: median $p$-values are ${<}0.001$ on both benchmarks. This indicates that the pairwise dependence structure among LLM judges is substantially non-Gaussian, likely reflecting the discrete three-category nature of winner labels and the heavy tail structure of judge agreement patterns.
475	
476	\paragraph{Ranking Robustness Despite Copula Rejection.} Despite the Gaussian copula being formally rejected, the closed-form $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (using $1 - \rho_{ij}^2$) and the plug-in MI $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (using $\exp(-2\,\widehat{\mathrm{MI}}(i,j))$ with Miller--Madow correction) produce highly concordant panel rankings within each $K$. Within-$K$ Spearman rank correlations are $\rho \approx 0.86$ ($K{=}3$: 0.862, $K{=}5$: 0.858, $K{=}7$: 0.867). The systematic level difference (plug-in $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ mean 3.38 vs.\ closed-form 4.29, averaged across $K$) reflects the Miller--Madow bias correction increasing MI estimates, which compresses $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI})$ toward zero. This level shift does not affect regression results, which depend on ranking.
477	
478	\paragraph{Implications.} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effectiveness as a predictor of panel vulnerability does not depend on the Gaussian copula being correct. The $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI})$ kernel provides a nonparametric, information-theoretic measure of pairwise alienation; the Gaussian copula merely offers a convenient closed-form approximation that preserves ranking fidelity. All regression analyses in the main text use the closed-form $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$; the plug-in MI version would yield equivalent conclusions for any analysis based on panel ordering.
479	
480	\section{Quantization Dummy Regression}
481	\label{app:quant_dummy}
482	
483	To disentangle the AWQ quantization confound from the diversity signal, we augment Equation~\ref{eq:regression} with a binary dummy $D_{\mathrm{quant}}$ indicating whether the panel's weakest judge is a quantized local model. The augmented regression is:
484	\begin{equation}
485	\log(\mathrm{ASR} + \epsilon) = \alpha + \beta_1 \log(K_{\mathrm{eff}}) + \beta_2 \log(\eta_{\max} + \epsilon) + \beta_3 D_{\mathrm{quant}}.
486	\end{equation}
487	Adding the dummy attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s standardized coefficient from $+0.39$ ($K{=}3$) to $+0.34$ ($K{=}5$) to $+0.27$ ($K{=}7$), with FDR-significant counts of 6/12, 6/12, and 4/12 respectively. The attenuation confirms partial confounding: some of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s explanatory power reflects the capability gap between quantized and API models rather than independence per se. However, collinearity between $D_{\mathrm{quant}}$ and both predictors escalates sharply with panel size (VIF${=}162$ at $K{=}7$), rendering the dummy unreliable for large panels. The quantization dummy analysis is therefore informative at $K{=}3$ (moderate VIF) but should be interpreted cautiously at $K{\geq}5$.
488	
489	\section{Bayesian Posterior Concentration Analysis}
490	\label{app:bayesian_entropy}
491	
492	Bayesian calibration neutralizes $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}7$ (0/12 significant; \S\ref{sec:analysis}). One hypothesized explanation is that the posterior concentrates on 2--3 accurate judges, compressing effective panel diversity. We test this by computing the posterior concentration ratio $C = \max_j w_j^{\mathrm{post}} / \min_j w_j^{\mathrm{post}}$ across panels.
493	
494	The concentration ratio declines modestly across panel sizes: $C = 0.890$ ($K{=}3$), $0.874$ ($K{=}5$), $0.867$ ($K{=}7$), a total change of only 2.3 percentage points. This gradual decline is insufficient to explain the abrupt neutralization at $K{=}7$ (from 6/12 at $K{=}3$ to 0/12 at $K{=}7$). The pattern is therefore not explained by simple posterior concentration; rather, the interaction between Bayesian weighting and the combinatorial structure of larger panels likely produces the neutralization. Full characterization of this interaction remains an open question.
495	
496	\section{Monte Carlo Calibration of Wild Cluster Bootstrap}
497	\label{app:monte_carlo}
498	
499	To verify that the wild cluster bootstrap does not over-reject under the null hypothesis, we conduct a Monte Carlo calibration study. For each of the 36 conditions (12 attack$\times$benchmark conditions $\times$ 3 panel sizes), we generate 1{,}000 permutation datasets by randomly shuffling the ASR outcome within each cluster, destroying any true association between predictors and ASR. For each permuted dataset, we run the wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}999$, Rademacher weights) and record whether the null is rejected at $\alpha{=}0.05$.
500	
501	Across all 36 conditions, the maximum empirical null rejection rate is 1.6\%, well below the nominal $\alpha{=}5\%$. The median rejection rate is 0.4\%. This confirms that the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative in our combinatorial design: the reported significance counts are lower bounds on what a correctly calibrated test would yield. The conservatism is expected given the low cluster counts (13, ${\leq}11$, ${\leq}9$ position clusters at $K{=}3,5,7$) and the use of Rademacher weights \citep{mackinnon2018wild}.
502	
503	\section{Near-Chance Model Sensitivity Analysis}
504	\label{app:near_chance}
505	
506	Two locally-hosted models (Qwen2.5-14B-AWQ, MMLU accuracy 0.253; Qwen2.5-72B-AWQ, 0.333) score near the 4-choice random baseline of 0.25. A natural concern is that the positive association between panel diversity ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) and attack success rate is mechanically driven by panels containing near-random judges, which simultaneously inflate both independence and vulnerability.
507	
508	We exclude all panels containing either model (MMLU ${<}\,0.35$ threshold), reducing the pool from 15 to 13 models and panel counts from 455/3{,}003/6{,}435 to 286/1{,}287/1{,}716 at $K{=}3/5/7$. We re-run OLS with BH-corrected $p$-values, the wild cluster bootstrap (WCB; $B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, max-$p$ across $K$ position clusters, BH-adjusted at $\alpha{=}0.05$), and the fractional logit GLM (Binomial, logit link, clustered SE with max-$p$, BH-adjusted). Table~\ref{tab:near_chance} reports FDR-significant $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ counts out of 12 conditions.
509	
510	\begin{table}[h]
511	\centering
512	\small
513	\caption{Near-chance model sensitivity: FDR-significant $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ conditions out of 12 (2 benchmarks $\times$ 6 attacks). $\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$: mean standardized $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient across conditions. The full-pool fractional logit count at $K{=}3$ (8/12) differs by $\pm 1$ from Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison} (9/12) due to clustering implementation details in the sensitivity script; this does not affect within-table comparisons.}
514	\label{tab:near_chance}
515	\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}
516	\toprule
517	$K$ & Panel set & $n$ & OLS & WCB & Frac & $\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$ & $\bar{R}^2$ \\
518	\midrule
519	3 & Full (15 models) & 455 & 11/12 & 3/12 & 8/12 & 0.349 & 0.340 \\
520	3 & Filtered (13 models) & 286 & 12/12 & 4/12 & 9/12 & \textbf{0.480} & 0.333 \\
521	\midrule
522	5 & Full (15 models) & 3{,}003 & 12/12 & 7/12 & 10/12 & 0.437 & 0.285 \\
523	5 & Filtered (13 models) & 1{,}287 & 11/12 & 7/12 & 11/12 & \textbf{0.512} & 0.256 \\
524	\midrule
525	7 & Full (15 models) & 6{,}435 & 12/12 & 8/12 & 10/12 & 0.401 & 0.244 \\
526	7 & Filtered (13 models) & 1{,}716 & 11/12 & 2/12 & 11/12 & 0.385 & 0.203 \\
527	\bottomrule
528	\end{tabular}
529	\end{table}
530	
531	At $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$, WCB significance is preserved or improved after filtering (3$\to$4 and 7$\to$7), and the mean standardized $\beta_{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \emph{increases} from 0.349 to 0.480 ($K{=}3$) and from 0.437 to 0.512 ($K{=}5$), indicating that the diversity--vulnerability association is stronger, not weaker, without near-chance models. Fractional logit significance likewise improves after filtering at every $K$ (8$\to$9, 10$\to$11, 10$\to$11), confirming that the effect is not an artifact of the GLM specification. At $K{=}7$, WCB significance drops from 8/12 to 2/12; however, the combinatorial filtering removes 73.3\% of panels (6{,}435$\to$1{,}716), substantially reducing statistical power for the conservative bootstrap test. The raw WCB $p$-values at $K{=}7$ cluster in the 0.02--0.15 range rather than reversing sign, and the standardized coefficient remains positive ($\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}{=}0.385$). The $K{=}7$ attenuation is therefore attributable to power loss from the sample reduction rather than absence of a genuine effect.
532	
533	In contrast, $\eta_{\max}$ WCB significance drops sharply after filtering (4$\to$1, 2$\to$0, 1$\to$0 at $K{=}3/5/7$), indicating that the near-chance models contribute disproportionately to the $\eta_{\max}$--ASR association: their high individual vulnerability inflates both $\eta_{\max}$ variation and the corresponding WCB significance. Overall, the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--ASR association is robust to excluding near-chance models at all panel sizes, while $\eta_{\max}$ is not.
534	
```
